is a cofibration;

•

a
weak-equivalence
if
L(f)
is a weak-equivalence;

•

an
acyclic cofibration
if
L(f)
is an acyclic cofibration (equivalently if
f
is both a cofibration and a weak-equivalence);

•

a
fibration
if it has the right lifting property with respect to acyclic cofibrations;

•

an
acyclic fibration
if it is both a cofibration and a weak-equivalence;

•

a
right cofibration-lifting
map is it has the right lifting property with respect to cofibrations.

We will refer to them as
the sets of maps in

\mathsf{C}

induced by the adjunction

L\dashv R
.

Proposition 73

(
[
MP89
]
)
.

There exists a small set
\mathcal{I}
of cofibrations of
\mathsf{C}
so that cofibrations are retracts of transfinite compositions of pushouts of maps in
\mathcal{I}
. Similarly, there exists a small set
\mathcal{J}
of acyclic cofibrations of
\mathsf{C}
so that acyclic cofibrations are retracts of transfinite compositions of pushouts of maps in
\mathcal{J}
.

We call
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
respectively the set of generating cofibrations and the set of generating acyclic cofibrations of
\mathsf{C}
.

Lemma 67
.

The sets of maps in
\mathsf{D}
induced by the adjunction
L\dashv R
satisfy the following properties.

(1)

The weak-equivalences, the cofibrations and the fibrations are stable through composition and retracts, and they contain all isomorphisms.

(2)

The weak-equivalences follow the 2-out-of-3 rule and the 2-out-of-6 rule.

(3)

Every square in
\mathsf{D}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

has a lifting whenever

(a)

f
is an acyclic cofibration and
g
is a fibration,

(b)

or
f
is a cofibration and
g
is a left right cofibration-lifting map.

(4)

Every map
f:X\longrightarrow Y
may be factored in a natural way as either

(a)

the composition of a cofibration followed by a right cofibration-lifting map,

(b)

or the composition of an acyclic cofibration map followed by a fibration.

(5)

Cofibrations are retracts of transfinite compositions of pushouts of generating cofibrations.

(6)

Left fibration-lifting maps are retracts of transfinite compositions of pushouts of generating acyclic cofibrations.

Proof.

This follows from a straightforward check, the small object argument and Proposition
73
.
∎

Theorem 16

(Left acyclicity condition,
[
BHK+15
]
)
.

The following assertions about the sets of maps in
\mathsf{C}
induced by the adjunction
L\dashv R
are equivalent.

(1)

These maps define a combinatorial model category structure on
\mathsf{C}
.

(2)

The set of right cofibration-lifting maps of
\mathsf{C}
is equal to the set of acyclic fibrations.

(3)

The set of right cofibration-lifting maps of
\mathsf{C}
is contained in the set of acyclic fibrations of
\mathsf{C}
.

(4)

The set of right cofibration-lifting maps of
\mathsf{C}
is contained in the set of weak-equivalences of
\mathsf{C}
.

Proof.

Clearly, (1) implies (2). Conversely, if (2) is satified, then by Lemma
, the sets of maps in
\mathsf{C}
induced by the adjunction
L\dashv R
are equivalent form a model category structure on
\mathsf{C}
. Therefore (1) and (2) are equivalent.

It is clear that (2) implies (3) and that (3) implies (4). Let us assume (4) and prove (2). Let
f:U\to Y
be an acylic fibration. Let us decompose
f:X\longrightarrow Y
as a cofibration
a:X\longrightarrow U
followed by a right cofibration-lifting map
b:U\longrightarrow Y
. By the 2-out-of-3 rule and since
b
and
f
are weak-equivalences,
a
is an acyclic cofibration. Thus the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

has a lifting
p
, which yields the following retract commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\penalty\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}}

Since the map
f
is a retract of
b
, it is a right cofibration-lifting map.
∎

Proposition 74

(
[
HKRS17
]
)
.

Let us suppose that

(1)

every object in
\mathsf{C}
has a natural cofibrant replacement functor, that is, there exists an endofunctor
Q
of
\mathsf{C}
together with a natural transformation
c:Q\longrightarrow\mathrm{Id}
so that for every object
X
,
Q(X)
is cofibrant and the map
Q(X)\longrightarrow X
is a weak-equivalence;

(2)

every cofibrant object
X
has a cylinder object, that is, the codiagonal map
X\sqcup X\longrightarrow X
may be factored by a cofibration followed by a weak-equivalence.

Then the sets of maps in
\mathsf{C}
induced by the adjunction
L\dashv R
endow
\mathsf{C}
with a model category structure.

Proof.

This follows from dual arguments as those used to prove Proposition
72
, using Theorem
16
.
∎
